One line per fact you will rely on, in the form "[where in the source] fact or number | use: core / diff / eval / icymi / limit". Include: every number the Evaluation section will cite; every design choice you plan to list under "In case you missed it"; and, for every claim you will judge (baseline fairness, novelty, measurement boundary), the paper's own wording or the named comparison behind it. End with one line "SUSPECT:" naming any headline number that looks off, or "none".
</notes>
<draft>
A complete first version of the document, in the DOCUMENT FORMAT below, written from the ledger: every number in it appears in the ledger.
</draft>
<check>
The audit of the draft. Part 1, format: for each numbered rule in the HARD FORMAT RULES write one line: the rule number, PASS or FAIL, and short evidence (for example "7 headings found, in order"). For each FAIL, add one line stating the exact fix.
Part 2, content, each as one line or a short list:
(C1) Name the three most load-bearing numbers or claims in Evaluation and conclusions, and for each give the ledger line and the source section it comes from, or write "not verified" and fix the draft.
(C2) For each "In case you missed it" entry, state which of the four tests in the SECTION GUIDE it passes and in a few words why it is not already covered elsewhere. Remove any that fail.
(C3) List every judging word from the TRACEABILITY paragraph that appears in the draft, with the paper's own wording that supports it, and list every place the draft attributes a result difference to a cause, with the paper's statement or "not stated". Remove or soften any without support.
(C4) State whether any statement in "My read" depends on a number or fact outside the source text; if so, mark it "not verified" or delete it.
(C5) List the seven section confidences and say why the spread is justified.
End with "Fixes to apply: N". Content fixes count as fixes.
</check>
<final>  (conditional -- write only when the check found fixes)
The document again, with every fix from the check applied. This block follows the DOCUMENT FORMAT exactly and contains only the document.
</final>

Write <final> ONLY when the check found fixes ("Fixes to apply: N" with N > 0). If the check reports "Fixes to apply: 0", do NOT write a <final> block: end the response right after </check>. The draft IS the document -- <final> would be identical to it, so writing it is unnecessary.

Each of <notes>, <draft> and <check> appears exactly once as an opening tag and once as a closing tag; <final> appears the same way only when the check found fixes, otherwise it is omitted entirely. Never write the strings <notes>, <draft>, <check>, or <final> inside any block's content. Write the notes first, then the draft, then the check, then the final (when written): do not claim a rule passes without citing evidence from the draft.

SECTION GUIDE (the seven sections, in this order)

Verdict (frontmatter). Two or three sentences: what the paper actually delivers, how far the evidence supports it, and where it is weakest. Specific enough that it could not describe a different paper.

Motivation. The problem and why the authors say it matters. End with one sentence on whether the motivation is itself convincing.

Core idea. What the method is, in the order a reimplementer would need it. Include the key equations the paper already contains. Large papers can contain several core ideas; whether something is a core idea or an "In case you missed it" entry is a judgement call, so make it and stay consistent. Embed the framework figure if one exists.

Distinguishing factor. Compare against each family of prior work separately, one short paragraph per family, naming the earlier idea when the paper itself makes it obvious. Separate "new idea" from "new packaging of a known idea". If the paper extends the authors' own earlier version, add a short "vs. their earlier version" paragraph using only what the paper says changed; otherwise say the paper describes none.

Evaluation and conclusions. Pick the results that carry the paper's claims (usually 2 to 5, plus any one surprising result). Write one paragraph per result. Each paragraph starts with a bold label naming the result, then leads with the result and how it was evaluated (benchmark, baselines, metric, scale, seeds), then gives "My read:" in a sentence or two on what that result does and does not show. Mention rigor (baselines, seeds, variance, custom metrics, different measurement boundaries) only where it changes how far that particular result can be trusted; do not audit every row. If two numbers the paper reports side by side undercut a headline claim, say so; that is reading, not cross-checking. Close with a paragraph starting "**Conclusion.**" giving the paper's own conclusion and your read of whether the evidence supports it, separating what the evidence supports from what it only suggests.

In case you missed it. Written for someone building on or reimplementing the work. These are concrete things the paper did or assumed that the other sections do not already cover: a design choice buried in the method, a hand-tuned or hard-coded part behind a general-sounding claim, an odd assumption or pruning rule and what it implies, a gap between the setting in the motivation and the setting actually tested, a clever side trick, a notable implementation detail. Test each candidate before including it:
- Is it a concrete thing the paper did, assumed, or left out, rather than a verdict on the paper? Verdicts belong in the verdict or the judgement table.
- Is it meta commentary (citation quality, venue, writing, novelty rhetoric)? Drop it.
- Is it already said in the other sections? Drop it.
- Is it a general critique? Then it belongs as one clause in a judgement-table answer, not here.
Three to six entries is typical. An empty section is better than a padded one.

Cautious judgement. Four rows, answers specific: name the baseline that is missing, the metric that is custom, the earlier work it echoes. Habits: note whether baselines are the strongest available and ran under the same budget, data, and tuning effort as far as the paper's own text shows; note when the paper defines its own figure of merit; note how many seeds, datasets, or workloads support a claim and whether variance is reported; prefer a stated uncertainty ("not verifiable from the main text", "looks suspicious, not checked") over a confident guess.

Sources and limits. One paragraph saying exactly what was read, what was not (appendix, code, supplementary, cited papers), and what could not be checked. State it once. Do not list citations.

DOCUMENT FORMAT
Produce a single Markdown document with YAML frontmatter for metadata and a markdown body for sections. No code fences, no prose before/after the frontmatter. No JSON. No string escaping needed: write plain Markdown.

---
title: Paper title
authors: Author1, Author2
venue: venue and id (e.g. "arXiv:2610.20359v1 [cs.ET]")
id: arxiv id or similar
whatRead: exactly what was read (full text / abstract only / main body without appendix)
verdict: 2-3 sentence verdict
overallConfidence: NN
---

## Motivation (Confidence: NN%)
...

## Core idea (Confidence: NN%)
Key equations written as $$ ... $$ (display) or \\( ... \\) (inline). Use raw LaTeX and write the delimiters yourself. Use \\\\ for line breaks only where the paper shows them. Figures as raw HTML: <figure data-n="N" data-a="ID"><figcaption>CAPTION</figcaption></figure> (N = figure index from the list above). Do not add an <img> tag: the pipeline adds the image or a link fallback.

## Distinguishing factor (Confidence: NN%)
...

## Evaluation and conclusions (Confidence: NN%)
**Result label.** Result and how it was evaluated. My read: ...

**Another result label.** ...

**Conclusion.** ...

## In case you missed it (Confidence: NN%)
- [pos] a positive entry
- [neg] a negative entry
- [neu] a neutral entry

## Cautious judgement (Confidence: NN%)
| Question | Answer | Confidence |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Fair comparison to external baselines? | ... | NN% |
| Invents its own FoM? | ... | NN% |
| Rewrite of an existing idea? | ... | NN% |
| Reliable enough to reuse? | ... | NN% |

## Sources and limits (Confidence: NN%)
One paragraph.

CONFIDENCE
Every section carries a percent confidence (0-100): your confidence that your reading of that section is right and complete, not a rating of the paper. Lower it when you saw only part of the text, when the source was garbled, when the paper is vague, or when judging requires baselines you did not verify. Spread should reflect real differences; do not default everything to 85%. For "Sources and limits" the number is your confidence that the statement of what was read and not read is complete, so it should not exceed 85 if any part of the source was garbled or missing. overallConfidence in the frontmatter is a bare integer 0-100.

HARD FORMAT RULES (verify each one in the check block)
1. Your response contains exactly one <notes>, one <draft> and one <check> block, in that order, plus one <final> block only when the check found fixes ("Fixes to apply: N" with N > 0), with nothing outside them.
2. Inside <final>, the first characters are "---" and the frontmatter closes with a second "---" line.
3. The frontmatter has exactly these keys in this order: title, authors, venue, id, whatRead, verdict, overallConfidence. overallConfidence is a bare integer. The verdict is 2-3 sentences. Commas in frontmatter values are followed by a space.
4. The body has exactly seven "## " headings, once each, in this order: Motivation, Core idea, Distinguishing factor, Evaluation and conclusions, In case you missed it, Cautious judgement, Sources and limits.
5. Every one of the seven headings ends with " (Confidence: NN%)" using a real number, for example "## Motivation (Confidence: 80%)".
6. The judgement table has a header row, a separator row, and exactly four data rows, with the four questions worded exactly as given and each Confidence cell a percentage.
7. Every entry in "In case you missed it" starts with "- [pos] ", "- [neg] ", or "- [neu] ", on its own line.
8. No code fences (triple backticks) anywhere in <draft> or <final>.
9. Every $$ is closed by a matching $$, and every \\( is closed by a matching \\).
10. Every <figure> has a data-n that appears in the figure list, a data-a equal to that figure's id, and a <figcaption>. There is no <img> tag. Embed at most two figures, and never one whose caption begins with "TABLE".
11. No JSON, no text before the frontmatter or after the last section, and the strings <notes>, <draft>, <check>, and <final> do not appear inside block contents.
12. Evaluation and conclusions contains no table. It has 3 to 7 paragraphs separated by blank lines; each begins with a bold label ("**...**"), every paragraph except the last contains "My read:", and the last begins with "**Conclusion.**".
13. Sources and limits contains exactly one prose paragraph, stating what was read once and not repeating it, and no list of citations.
14. The seven section confidences contain at least three distinct values, and none exceeds 90.
15. Every number cited in Evaluation and conclusions appears in the ledger.`
}

=== src/render.js ===

/**
 * Pure digest Markdown -> HTML renderer. No filesystem, no network: safe to use
 * in Node, in a browser, and inside Obsidian dataviewjs.
 *
 * Renders a digest object (as produced by src/digest.js from the agent's
 * Markdown response) into assets/template.html markup using `marked` for
 * Markdown parsing.
 */

import { marked } from "marked";

const ESC = { "&": "&amp;", "<": "&lt;", ">": "&gt;", '"': "&quot;" };

export function esc(s) {
  return String(s == null ? "" : s).replace(/[&<>"']/g, (c) => ESC[c] || c);
}

/**
 * Render a section's markdown content to HTML.
 *
 * Protects math ($$...$$ and \(...\)) so marked does not touch it, then
 * runs marked. Figure blocks (<figure data-n="...">...</figure>) pass
 * through as raw HTML so the browser-side template script can turn each
 * figure[data-n] into a lazyloaded image with a link fallback.
 */
function renderContent(content) {
  const t = String(content || "");
  const mathBlocks = [];
  let p = t;

  // Protect display math $$...$$ as placeholders so marked leaves them alone.
  p = p.replace(/\$\$([\s\S]*?)\$\$/g, (_, math) => {
    const idx = mathBlocks.length;
    mathBlocks.push(`\\[${math}\\]`);
    return `\x00MATH${idx}\x00`;
  });

  // Protect inline math \(...\) so marked leaves it alone.
  // Use RegExp constructor to avoid literal-slash escaping issues in regex literals.
  const inlineRe = new RegExp("\\\\\\(([\\s\\S]*?)\\\\\\)", "g");
  p = p.replace(inlineRe, (_, math) => {
    const idx = mathBlocks.length;
    mathBlocks.push(`\\(${math}\\)`);
    return `\x00MATH${idx}\x00`;
  });

  // Protect inline math $...$ (single dollar) so marked leaves it alone.
  // Agents default to $...$ for inline equations, so account for it just like
  // \( ... \). The content may not contain a raw `$` (that would be a separate
  // inline math or display math delimiter), which keeps multiple $...$ pairs on
  // one line from being swallowed into a single match.
  const singleDollarRe = /\$([^$]+)\$/g;
  p = p.replace(singleDollarRe, (_, math) => {
    const idx = mathBlocks.length;
    mathBlocks.push(`\\(${math}\\)`);
    return `\x00MATH${idx}\x00`;
  });

  const html = marked.parse(p);

  // Restore math placeholders. Escape < and & (and quotes) inside the
  // restored math so an equation can never break the surrounding HTML;
  // MathJax reads the unescaped text from the DOM afterwards.
  let out = html;
  for (let i = 0; i < mathBlocks.length; i++) {
    out = out.replace(`\x00MATH${i}\x00`, () => esc(mathBlocks[i]));
  }
  return out;
}

function confidenceChip(conf) {
  const c = conf == null ? 0 : Number(conf);
  return `<span class="conf">${c}%</span>`;
}

function chipSign(tag) {
  return { pos: "+", neg: "−", neu: "·" }[tag] || "·";
}

function renderSection(s) {
  const conf = confidenceChip(s.confidence);
  let body = "";

  switch (s.id) {
    case "mot":
    case "diff":
    case "core":
      body = `<div class="prose">${renderContent(s.content || "")}</div>`;
      break;
    case "eval":
      // Evaluation and conclusions: one .item per top-level paragraph (each
      // starts with a bold label), flat blocks with no block children.
      body = (s.content || "")
        .split(/\n\n+/)
        .map((p) => p.trim())
        .filter(Boolean)
        .map((p) => `<div class="item">${renderParagraph(p)}</div>`)
        .join("");
      break;
    case "icymi":
      body =
        (s.entries || []).map(
          (e) =>
            `<div class="item"><span class="chip ${esc(
              e.tag || "neu"
            )}">${chipSign(e.tag)}</span>${esc(e.text)}</div>`
        ).join("");
      break;
    case "judge":
      body =
        `<div class="wrap"><table><thead><tr><th>Question</th><th>Answer</th><th>Conf.</th></tr></thead><tbody>` +
        (s.rows || []).map(
          (r) =>
            `<tr><td>${esc(r.question)}</td><td>${esc(r.answer)}</td><td>${
              r.conf == null ? "" : r.conf
            }%</td></tr>`
        ).join("") +
        `</tbody></table></div>`;
      break;
    default:
      body = `<div class="prose">${renderContent(s.content || "")}</div>`;
  }
  return `<h2 class="s" id="${esc(s.id)}">${esc(s.title)}${conf}</h2>${body}`;
}

/**
 * Render one paragraph of markdown without a wrapping <p>, so the result
 * stays a flat block (safe as the sole content of .item, li or td).
 */
function renderParagraph(content) {
  const html = renderContent(content).trim();
  const m = /^<p>([\s\S]*)<\/p>$/.exec(html);
  return m ? m[1] : html;
}

/**
 * Render a digest object into a complete HTML document string.
 *
 * @param {object} digest - parsed digest object (metadata + sections)
 * @param {object} [source] - resolved source (figures, baseUrl) for the figure pipeline
 * @returns {string} HTML
 */
export function render(digest, source = {}) {
  const figures = Array.isArray(source.figures) ? source.figures : [];
  const base = source.baseUrl || "";

  const map = {};   // n -> image URL (for  or <object data> figures)
  const svgByN = {}; // n -> serialised inline <svg> markup (inlined server-side)
  figures.forEach((f, i) => {
    const key = String(i + 1);
    if (f.svg) {
      svgByN[key] = f.svg;
    } else if (f.url) {
      map[key] = f.url;
    }
  });
  const mapJson = JSON.stringify(map);

  const navLinks = (digest.sections || [])
    .map((s) => `<a class="block py-1" href="#${esc(s.id)}">${esc(s.title)}</a>`)
    .join("");

  const sectionsHtml = (digest.sections || []).map((s) => renderSection(s)).join("");

  // Scale an inlined SVG to the column width: give it a viewBox derived
  // from its width/height (so the content scales) and let it fill the
  // figure container.
  function scaleSvg(svg) {
    return String(svg).replace(/<svg\b([^>]*)>/, (m, attrs) => {
      let a = attrs;
      if (!/viewBox\b/.test(a)) {
        const wM = /width\s*=\s*"(\d+)"/.exec(a);
        const hM = /height\s*=\s*"(\d+)"/.exec(a);
        if (wM && hM) a += ` viewBox="0 0 ${wM[1]} ${hM[1]}"`;
      }
      a = a.replace(/\bwidth\s*=\s*"[^"]*"/, 'width="100%"');
      a = a.replace(/\bheight\s*=\s*"[^"]*"/, 'height="auto"');
      if (!/style\s*=/i.test(a)) {
        a += ' style="display:block"';
      } else {
        a = a.replace(/style="([^"]*)"/, 'style="$1;display:block"');
      }
      return `<svg${a}>`;
    });
  }

  // Figures: inline server-side SVG or raster images; everything else gets
  // the client-side link fallback via BASE/MAP.
  let sectionsWithImages = sectionsHtml;
  const figureRe = /<figure\b[^>]*>([\s\S]*?)<\/figure>/g;
  sectionsWithImages = sectionsWithImages.replace(figureRe, (match, inner) => {
    const nMatch = /data-n="([^"]+)"/.exec(match);
    if (!nMatch) return match;
    const n = nMatch[1];

    const svg = svgByN[n];
    if (svg) {
      // Inline SVG figure (from the paper's HTML); the client-side script
      // must not clobber it, so it skips figures that already have content.
      return match.replace(">", `>${scaleSvg(svg)}`);
    }

    if (/<img\b/.test(inner)) return match; // already has an image
    const url = map[n] || "";
    if (!url) return match; // no image and no SVG: client script adds the link fallback
    const img = `<img loading="lazy" alt="Figure ${n}" src="${esc(url)}">`;
    return match.replace(">", `>${img}`);
  });

  const mapJs = Object.entries(map)
    .map(([k, v]) => `    ${JSON.stringify(k)}: ${JSON.stringify(v)},`)
    .join("\n");

  // assets/template.html is read separately and placeholders replaced here.
  const placeholders = {
    "{{TITLE}}": esc(digest.title || ""),
    "{{AUTHORS}}": esc(Array.isArray(digest.authors) ? digest.authors.join(", ") : String(digest.authors || "")),
    "{{VENUE}}": esc(digest.venue || ""),
    "{{ID}}": esc(digest.id || ""),
    "{{WHAT_READ}}": esc(digest.whatRead || ""),
    "{{VERDICT}}": esc(digest.verdict || ""),
    "{{OVERALL_CONF}}": esc(digest.overallConfidence || 0),
    "{{SECTIONS}}": sectionsWithImages,
    "{{NAV_LINKS}}": navLinks,
    "{{BASE}}": esc(base),
    "{{MAP}}": mapJson,
  };

  return { placeholders, mapJs };
}

export { esc as escapeHtml };

=== src/template.js ===

/**
 * Template stage: render a digest JSON object into assets/template.html and
 * write the self-contained HTML file to disk.
 *
 * The pure render logic lives in src/render.js (browser / Obsidian safe);
 * this module only adds filesystem I/O.
 */

import { readFile, writeFile, mkdir } from "fs/promises";
import { join } from "path";
import { render, esc } from "./render.js";

const TEMPLATE_PATH = join(process.cwd(), "assets", "template.html");

let templateCache = null;

async function loadTemplate() {
  if (!templateCache) {
    templateCache = await readFile(TEMPLATE_PATH, "utf8");
  }
  return templateCache;
}

/**
 * Render the digest JSON into a full HTML document string.
 *
 * @param {object} digest - parsed digest object
 * @param {object} [source] - resolved source (figures, baseUrl)
 * @returns {Promise<string>} HTML
 */
export async function renderString(digest, source = {}) {
  const tmpl = await loadTemplate();
  const { placeholders, mapJs } = render(digest, source);

  let html = tmpl;
  for (const [token, value] of Object.entries(placeholders)) {
    html = html.split(token).join(value);
  }
  html = html.replace("const MAP = {{MAP}};", `const MAP = {\n${mapJs}\n  };`);
  return html;
}

/**
 * Render the digest JSON and write the HTML file to disk.
 *
 * @param {object} digest
 * @param {object} [source]
 * @param {string} [outDir] - default "outputs"
 * @returns {Promise<string>} output path
 */
export async function renderFile(digest, source = {}, outDir = "outputs") {
  const html = await renderString(digest, source);
  await mkdir(outDir, { recursive: true });
  const base = outputFilenameBase(digest, source);
  const outPath = join(outDir, base + ".html");
  await writeFile(outPath, html, "utf8");
  return outPath;
}

function outputFilenameBase(digest, source = {}) {
  const author = sanitize((digest.authors && digest.authors[0]) || "Unknown");
  const year = extractYear(digest, source);
  const journal = venueAbbv(digest);
  const title = shortenedTitle(digest.title || "");
  const name = `${author}-${year}-${journal}-${title}`;
  return name.slice(0, 100);
}

function sanitize(field) {
  return String(field)
    .normalize("NFD")
    .replace(/[\u0300-\u036f]/g, "")
    .replace(/[^A-Za-z0-9 _.\-]/g, "")
    .replace(/[_\s]+/g, "_")
    .replace(/-+/g, "-")
    .replace(/^[-.]+/, "")
    .replace(/[-.]+$/, "");
}

function shortenedTitle(title) {
  const stopwords = new Set([
    "a", "an", "the", "of", "for", "in", "on", "and", "with", "to", "via",
    "is", "are",
  ]);
  const beforeColon = String(title).split(":")[0];
  const words = beforeColon
    .toLowerCase()
    .split(/\s+/)
    .filter((w) => w && !stopwords.has(w));
  return words.slice(0, 4).join("_");
}

function extractYear(digest, source) {
  const m = String(digest.venue || "").match(/\b(19|20)\d{2}\b/);
  if (m) return m[0];
  const idM = String(digest.id || source.value || "").match(/(\d{2})\d{2}\.\d+/);
  if (idM) return `20${idM[1]}`;
  return String(new Date().getFullYear());
}

function venueAbbv(digest) {
  const v = String(digest.venue || "");
  if (/arxiv/i.test(v)) return "arXiv";
  const known = [
    "neurips", "icml", "iclr", "isscc", "jssc", "tcad", "cvpr", "iccv",
    "eccv", "acl", "emnlp", "naacl",
  ];
  const low = v.toLowerCase();
  for (const k of known) if (low.includes(k)) return k.charAt(0).toUpperCase() + k.slice(1);
  const tok = v.split(/\s+/)[0];
  return sanitize(tok) || "arXiv";
}

export { esc, outputFilenameBase, sanitize, shortenedTitle, extractYear, venueAbbv };

=== test/examples/digest.md ===

<notes>
[Abstract] KV260 at 200 MHz: 76.57% CIFAR-10, 91.85% SVHN, ~4.20 W mean module power | use: eval
[Abstract] Effective throughput 31.22 and 31.57 GMAC/s | use: eval
[Abstract/IV-B] Energy vs same integer method on Jetson: 40.4% and 42.1% lower, runtime 1.86x and 1.82x longer, "respective platform power domains" | use: eval
[Intro] Energy 19.62 and 32.01 kJ | use: eval
[IV-A] FPGA VCC_SOM vs Jetson VDD_IN different module power boundaries; no idle subtraction; Jetson PyTorch, 25 W mode | use: eval/limit
[IV-B] 30.49% and 30.83% of 102.4 GMAC/s peak; PL is 97.1% and 96.8% of time; stall attribution not separated | use: eval
[IV-B] Checks: selected states plus prediction counts 1,645,036 and 3,796,354, test 7,657/10,000 and 23,910/26,032; "not every intermediate value" | use: eval
[IV-C] Regional errors 64-256 KiB vs 8-16 MiB expanded, 64-256x; capacity not traffic | use: eval
[IV-C] 448 MiB raw-output payload avoided per full batch; RTL 65,614 vs 131,157 cycles (50.0%); throttled 114,788 vs 316,886 (63.8%); isolated modules | use: eval
[IV-D] Integer local vs FP32 BP: energy -23.8% / -27.0%, accuracy -3.30 / -0.53 pts | use: eval
[IV-D] Vs Local FP32: higher accuracy, 25.8% lower peak CUDA, 2.10x / 2.25x longer; "results reflect both training and implementation choices" | use: eval
[IV-D] Freezing: time -28.4% / -32.3%, accuracy +6.38 / +2.03 pts; History vs Current within 0.13 pts | use: icymi
[V] One FPGA seed, logical storage, isolated conversion tests | use: limit
[II-A] Quadrant-averaged local classifiers, 4CK weights | use: core
[II-C] Histogram 99.9th percentile, 4-batch window, 8 warmup batches | use: core
[II-E] GB5 gradients with max|G|≤1 suppressed; INT16 master state only for final classifier | use: icymi
[III-C/IV-C] 161.22 MiB DDR reserved; 64 MiB warmup buffer stays; two 16 MiB error banks stay | use: icymi
[II-D] Fixed 2^15 scale for e≤-10 in TiLoss | use: icymi
[IV-A] SVHN omits last image; slack +0.003 ns; eval target = max of last four candidates | use: icymi
[IV-B] Table IV prior work: six layers 16/16/32/32/64/64, backprop; ours differs in network and training | use: diff
[II-D] TiLoss from NITI public implementation | use: diff
SUSPECT: none
</notes>

<draft>
---
title: LocalTrain: Integer-Only Local Learning for Energy-Efficient CNN Training on Edge FPGAs
authors: Shiqi Ding, Xuanyu Liang, Xinyang Liu, Boyang Li, Zhiqiang Que, Guosheng Hu, Jiayang Li
venue: arXiv:2610.17099v1
id: 2610.17099
whatRead: arXiv HTML full text main body (Tables I-V and figure images not present in the extracted text, references partly garbled)
verdict: The paper delivers a working KV260 prototype that trains a four-layer CNN with local classifiers using INT8 operands, and reports 40.4% and 42.1% lower measured energy than the same integer method on a Jetson Orin Nano. The evidence is a single FPGA seed on two small datasets, and the energy comparison crosses different power-measurement boundaries. The mechanism claims (compact errors, tile folding, historical scales) are supported by storage accounting and isolated RTL tests rather than board-level ablations.
overallConfidence: 72
---

## Motivation (Confidence: 74%)
The authors argue that on-device training must cut computation, intermediate storage and data movement, since backpropagation retains forward inputs or recomputes them. Local learning shortens activation lifetimes and low precision shrinks storage per value, but the paper says existing algorithmic results do not show how convolution and local-learning operations should share hardware or how intermediate data should be stored and reused. My read: the gap is plausible and the target is concrete, but the task is small CIFAR-10 and SVHN training, so the motivation is only partly tested on realistic personalization workloads.

## Core idea (Confidence: 78%)
The network has four bias-free 3x3 layers, alternating standard and grouped convolutions, each followed by requantization and ReLU, with max pooling after layers 2 and 4, plus a final classifier. Each layer gets a trainable local classifier on its quantized ReLU outputs; each channel is averaged over four equal spatial regions, giving 4C features and 4CK weights. With regional features F and pre-update classifier weights V_pre, all three needed products are integer matrix products:

$$Z=FV_{\mathrm{pre}}^{T},\qquad G_{V}=E^{T}F,\qquad E_{F}=EV_{\mathrm{pre}}$$

E comes from an integer TiLoss. Regional errors are broadcast back over their region with a 1/A factor applied as an exponent shift, masked by the ReLU, and used to train the associated layer only. Tensors are integer codes with a shared exponent, and INT32 accumulators are converted to INT8 by a power-of-two shift:

$$q_{i}^{\prime}=\operatorname{sat}_{8}\left(\operatorname{RN}(q_{i}2^{-s})\right),\qquad e^{\prime}=e+s,\quad s\geq 0$$

Convolution output scales are historical. After eight warmup batches, the shift comes from the maximum of the last four batch candidates, each taken from the 99.9th percentile of a histogram of raw INT32 magnitudes:

$$e_{\mathrm{target},t}=\max_{i=1,\ldots,4}h_{t-i},\qquad s_{t}=\max(0,e_{\mathrm{target},t}-e_{\mathrm{raw},t})$$

Gradients are summed over the logical batch, then normalized by the full-tensor maximum (GB5), and the update is applied in the code domain:

$$s_{G}=\max(0,b(\max|G|)-5),\qquad g=\operatorname{sat}_{8}\left(\operatorname{RN}(G2^{-s_{G}})\right),\qquad W^{+}=\operatorname{sat}_{8}(W-g)$$

On hardware, eight pods each accumulate 8x8 INT32 tiles from INT8 vector pairs (512 MACs per cycle, 102.4 GMAC/s peak at 200 MHz). Compact regional errors are held in DDR and expanded on chip only for convolutional weight gradients. Partial gradients (32 per layer at B=256) are folded one 8x8 tile at a time in a 48-bit accumulator before the GB5 pass.

<figure data-n="1" data-a="S2.F1"><figcaption>Fig. 1: One CNN layer and its local supervision. Dashed paths retain operands; the local head also updates VV. No convolutional input gradient is sent to earlier layers.</figcaption></figure>

<figure data-n="2" data-a="S3.F2"><figcaption>Fig. 2: Hardware organization. Convolution and classifier operations reuse the matrix engine. Regional errors expand in operand supply; numerical and output units share DDR access. Control is dashed; data connections are solid.</figcaption></figure>

## Distinguishing factor (Confidence: 68%)
**FPGA backpropagation accelerators.** The paper cites prior designs that share compute across forward, backward and weight-gradient passes. Its difference is local supervision through a classifier per layer, which removes cross-layer error propagation but adds classifier work, mapped onto the same matrix engine. The shared-engine idea itself is a known one; the new part is mapping local-classifier score, error and gradient operations onto it.

**Integer training.** The error generator is an adaptation of the public NITI TiLoss, and GB5 normalization is also attributed to NITI. NITRO-D and FF-INT8 are cited as GPU demonstrations of integer local training. What the paper adds is an FPGA realization with its own numerical changes (lookup tables, a shared reciprocal, a fixed polynomial scale).

**Historical scaling.** Scale selection from earlier batches follows cited prior work (Yamaguchi et al., Flexpoint). The paper's own policy is a histogram percentile with a four-batch window, and the hardware contribution is conversion that does not wait for current-output statistics. This is a packaging of a known idea into the output path.

**Local classifiers.** The regional-average classifier follows Belilovsky et al.; the paper's addition is keeping errors compact and expanding them in operand supply.

**vs. their earlier version.** The paper describes none.

## Evaluation and conclusions (Confidence: 65%)
**Complete training and energy against Jetson.** On the KV260 at 200 MHz, the prototype reaches 76.57% on CIFAR-10 and 91.85% on SVHN at about 4.20 W mean module power, using 19.62 and 32.01 kJ. Against the same integer method on a Jetson Orin Nano (25 W mode), measured energy is 40.4% and 42.1% lower, with 1.86x and 1.82x longer runtime. My read: the headline is a real measured comparison, but the paper states FPGA VCC_SOM and Jetson VDD_IN are different module power boundaries, power is not idle-subtracted, and there is one FPGA seed. The size of the gap is therefore not a clean like-for-like energy ratio.

**Functional agreement.** First-batch and checkpoint-restoration checks match software states, and FPGA and Jetson integer runs share cumulative correct training predictions (1,645,036 and 3,796,354) and final test counts (7,657/10,000 and 23,910/26,032). My read: matching complete-run counts is a strong aggregate check, but the paper itself says it does not cover every intermediate value.

**Effective throughput.** Aggregate effective throughput is 31.22 GMAC/s on CIFAR-10 and 31.57 on SVHN, 30.49% and 30.83% of the 102.4 GMAC/s peak, with PL execution at 97.1% and 96.8% of training time. The authors attribute short-reduction layers to a result-bandwidth constraint but say counters do not separate memory stalls from backpressure. My read: the utilization number is honest and uses nominal algorithmic MACs, but the cause of the 70% shortfall is a hypothesis in the paper, not a measurement.

**Storage and conversion savings.** At B=256, regional errors take 64-256 KiB per layer against 8-16 MiB for expanded INT8 maps (64-256x), and direct conversion avoids 448 MiB of raw-output write/read payload per full batch. In an isolated RTL test with 262,144 INT32 values, direct conversion needs 65,614 versus 131,157 cycles (50.0%), and 114,788 versus 316,886 cycles (63.8%) under a throttling model. My read: these are logical capacities and isolated-module cycle counts; the paper states they are distinct from peak live storage and measured DDR traffic, and the reserved DDR workspace stays at 161.22 MiB, so board-level benefit is not shown.

**Software comparison with FP32.** Integer local training uses 23.8% and 27.0% less energy than FP32 backpropagation on Jetson, with accuracy 3.30 and 0.53 points lower. Against Local FP32 it has higher accuracy and 25.8% lower peak CUDA allocation but is 2.10x and 2.25x slower; the paper notes Local FP32 uses optimized GPU operators and that results reflect both training and implementation choices. My read: the accuracy cost on CIFAR-10 is visible and the speed comparison says little about the algorithm.

**Conclusion.** The paper concludes that the prototype shows complete integer local CNN training with shared arithmetic, lower measured energy and longer runtime than Jetson. The evidence supports a functioning, measured system whose numerics reproduce a software reference at the aggregate level. It only suggests that compact errors, tile folding and historical scales are what produce the gains, since no board-level ablations are reported, which the authors list as future work.

## In case you missed it (Confidence: 62%)
- [neg] The 64 MiB raw-output warmup buffer and two 16 MiB error-bank allocations stay reserved after warmup, so reserved DDR is 161.22 MiB despite the compact-error and direct-conversion claims.
- [neu] Convolution and local-classifier updates are done in the code domain without aligning the gradient exponent to the fixed weight exponent, and gradients with max|G| of 1 or less are suppressed.
- [neu] Only the final classifier has an INT16 master state (M=16W) to keep sub-step updates; other layers do not.
- [neu] The TiLoss variant uses a fixed 2^15 scale for score exponents e of -10 or below, and one reciprocal per sample with 28 fractional bits for normalization.
- [neu] Evaluation fixes each layer's scale to the maximum of its last four training candidates, and the policy study notes Current scaling uses unlabeled statistics from each test batch.
- [neg] Scheduled freezing (layers freeze after 20/20/30/40 epochs) changes accuracy as well as time (+6.38 and +2.03 points in the study), so reported accuracy and energy partly depend on a hand-set schedule.
- [neu] SVHN training omits the last image to fit eight-sample grouping, and routed timing closes with +0.003 ns setup slack.

The prior FPGA systems use different networks and backpropagation, and the paper says accuracy differences include network and training choices; Table IV numbers were not in the extracted text. | 62% |

---

## title: Closed-Form Optimal Weight Splitting for Matrix–Vector Multiply on Noisy In-Memory Accelerators
authors: Mehmet Aktukmak, David A. Roberts
venue: arXiv:2610.20359v1 [cs.ET]
id: 2610.20359v1
whatRead: arXiv HTML full text
verdict: The paper fills a critical gap in analog in-memory computing (AIMC) weight-slicing theory by incorporating a finite-grid quantization floor alongside additive output noise, deriving a simple closed-form optimal base $b_{\mathrm{opt}}$. Although the additive noise assumption idealizes complex device non-idealities, the resulting interior optimum ($b_{\mathrm{opt}} \approx 5\text{–}6$) demonstrates robustness across full device-physics crossbar simulations and LLM evaluation benchmarks.
overallConfidence: 90

## Motivation (Confidence: 90%)

In analog in-memory computing (AIMC), matrix–vector multiplication (MVM) is executed directly in crossbar arrays via Kirchhoff's laws. Because physical memory cells (e.g., PCM, RRAM) suffer from low per-cell precision and read noise, a standard mitigation is weight splitting across $n_W$ physical cells using a place-value radix/base $b$. This choice exposes a fundamental tension: a large base provides fine resolution but exponentially amplifies the high-significance cell's noise in the summed bit-line current, whereas a base of one ($b=1$, equal-fill duplication) averages noise out but yields zero additional resolution.

Prior analytical work (e.g., Le Gallo et al.) modeled device noise in isolation, yielding a monotonic error curve that strictly favored $b=1$. Practical implementations were thus forced to rely on empirical sweeps or per-device search heuristics. The authors set out to construct a closed-form model that incorporates both finite-grid quantization and device noise, enabling designers to compute the optimal weight-splitting base directly from a single normalized device-noise measurement $\eta$.

**My read:** The motivation is sound and relevant. As AIMC moves toward edge LLM inference and scientific computing, analytical tools that bypass costly per-device heuristic searches are highly practical.

## Core Idea (Confidence: 92%)

The paper formalizes low-precision AIMC weight representation as a custom fixed-point quantization problem with additive hardware noise. For a cell precision of $q$ signed bits ($\mathrm{bound} = 2^{q-1}-1$), a weight $w$ mapped across $n_W=2$ cells is encoded as:

$$\hat{w} = \mathrm{scale} \cdot (s_L + b \, s_H), \quad s_H, s_L \in [-\mathrm{bound}, \mathrm{bound}]$$

where $\mathrm{scale} = W_{\max} / [\mathrm{bound}(1+b)]$.

Assuming additive per-tile read noise $n_k \sim \mathcal{N}(0, \sigma_{\mathrm{out}}^2)$ and uniform master-grid quantization error $\Delta^2 / 12$, the total relative MVM mean squared error simplifies to:

$$\mathrm{relMSE}(b) = k_{\sigma}^2 \eta^2 \frac{1+b^2}{(1+b)^2} + \frac{k_{\sigma}^2}{12 \, \mathrm{bound}^2 (1+b)^2}$$

where $\eta$ is the per-tile output noise normalized by the signal RMS. Differentiating $\mathrm{relMSE}(b)$ with respect to $b$ yields the interior continuous optimum in closed form:

$$b_{\mathrm{opt}} = 1 + \frac{1}{12 \, \mathrm{bound}^2 \, \eta^2}$$

capped at $2\,\mathrm{bound}+1$.

To ensure gap-free representation without low-digit saturation, the base satisfies $b \le \mathrm{bound} + 1$. Additionally, the authors introduce a fixed random block-diagonal orthogonal rotation applied to the weight matrix $W$ before mapping, which acts as incoherence preconditioning to drive operand distributions toward Gaussian and homogenize per-tile noise profiles.

## Distinguishing Factor (Confidence: 90%)

* **vs. Le Gallo et al. [1]:** Le Gallo et al. focused on state-dependent (multiplicative) PCM noise without unifying the quantization floor into the base optimization objective, which naturally resulted in a monotonic preference for $b=1$. Aktukmak and Roberts show that under additive tile output noise, an interior optimum $b_{\mathrm{opt}} > 1$ emerges. Le Gallo's $b=1$ result is shown to be the noise-dominated limit ($\eta \to \infty$) of the authors' generalized formula.
* **vs. Empirical/Evolutionary Search (Mackin et al. [2], Diware et al. [10, 11]):** Rather than running evolutionary algorithms or per-model grid searches to determine bit-significance factors, this work provides an immediate, zero-overhead closed-form calculation derived from a single physical device calibration parameter.
* **vs. Rotated Non-Uniform Encodings (SpinQuant, QuIP# [16, 17]):** While leveraging orthogonal rotation principles, this work targets the multi-cell significance base for uniform physical cell steps rather than learning non-uniform Lloyd-Max codebooks per tile.

## Evaluation and Conclusions (Confidence: 88%)

The authors evaluate their optimal base prediction across three distinct fidelity tiers:

1. **Analytic Monte Carlo (Fig. 2):** Evaluated across noise levels $\eta \in \{0.02, 0.05, 0.10\}$. The closed-form expression matches Monte Carlo simulations within ~1% inside the validity range $b \le \mathrm{bound} + 1$, correctly predicting SNR peaks at $b_{\mathrm{opt}} \in \{5.25, 1.68, 1.17\}$.
* **My read:** The analytic match is clean within the validity bound. Beyond $b > \mathrm{bound} + 1$, the Monte Carlo curves diverge upward due to low-cell saturation, precisely as the theory predicts.


2. **Device-Physics Crossbar Simulation (Fig. 3):** Evaluated on a 512×512 differential 4-bit crossbar array model incorporating non-additive IR-drop network solvers, ADC quantization, and programming noise across Gemma-3 270M and Llama-3.2 1B layers.
* **My read:** Despite non-additive physical effects, the predicted interior peak survives intact. SNR peaks at $b=5$, delivering a +1.6 dB gain over equal-fill ($b=1$) and a +3.3 to +3.4 dB improvement over standard base-16 bit-slicing. The broad, flat basin ($b \in [3, 8]$) confirms that integer rounding of $b_{\mathrm{opt}}$ incurs negligible loss.



3. **End-Application Benchmarks (Table I):** Evaluated on scientific MVM operators (2D Laplacian stencil, ill-conditioned matrix) and transformer zero-shot accuracy (8 LM-Eval harness tasks).
* **My read:** On Gemma-270M and Llama-1B, base-$N$ split at $b=6$ achieves +13.1% and +9.0% accuracy improvements over equal-fill ($b=1$). This matches the accuracy of residual redundancy (+12.3% and +7.7%) within run-to-run noise ($\pm 0.008$), while operating strictly on uniform cell placements without per-tile codebook overhead.



**Paper's conclusion vs. My read:** The authors conclude that optimal weight splitting under additive noise is governed by a simple, closed-form resolution-noise tradeoff that supersedes the prior $b=1$ convention. My read is that the experimental evidence strongly supports this claim for additive-limited crossbars. However, practical hardware often exhibits a hybrid mix of additive and state-dependent multiplicative noise, meaning deployed systems will operate on a spectrum between $b_{\mathrm{opt}}$ and $1$.

## In Case You Missed It (Confidence: 85%)

* `[pos]` Shared orthogonal preconditioning homogenizes weight distributions across tiles, concentrating 92–97% of optimal per-tile bases within $b \in \{4, 5, 6\}$ and removing the need for fine-grained per-tile base tuning.
* `[neu]` Signed 4-bit cells ($\mathrm{bound}=7$) with two cells per weight ($n_W=2$) establish an unsaturated validity ceiling of $b \le 8$.
* `[neg]` The closed-form derivation assumes purely additive tile output noise; conductance-proportional noise, state retention drift, and non-linear conductance tuning errors are omitted from the primary formula.
* `[neu]` Increasing the cell budget to $n_W \ge 3$ causes the quantization floor to collapse rapidly ($S = (b^{n_W}-1)/(b-1)$), shifting the theoretical optimum toward lower integer bases ($b_{\mathrm{opt}} \to 2$).

## Cautious Judgement (Confidence: 90%)

| Question | Answer | Confidence |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Fair comparison to external baselines? | Yes; compared against equal-fill ($b=1$), base-16 nibble slicing, rotated non-uniform grids, and residual redundancy at an identical 2-cell budget. | 90% |
| Invents its own FoM? | No; relies on standard relative MVM MSE, SNR in dB, and standard LM-Evaluation-Harness zero-shot accuracy metrics. | 95% |
| Rewrite of an existing idea? | No; unifies the slicing noise expression from Le Gallo et al. [1] with finite-grid quantization to derive a novel interior optimal base in closed form. | 90% |
| Reliable enough to reuse? | Yes; closed-form predictions are analytically verified and verified against a comprehensive device-physics crossbar simulator. | 90% |

## Sources and Limits (Confidence: 92%)

* **What was read:** The full text and figures of arXiv:2610.20359v1 [cs.ET] (arXiv HTML format).
* **What was not read/checked:** No supplementary code repository was inspected. Mathematical proofs and algebraic steps were not re-derived line-by-line per standard second-pass reading scope. External references ([1], [2]) were not separately fetched.

=== test/fixtures/digest.md ===

<draft>
---
title: LocalTrain: Integer-Only Local Learning for Energy-Efficient CNN Training on Edge FPGAs
authors: Shiqi Ding, Xuanyu Liang, Xinyang Liu, Boyang Li, Zhiqiang Que, Guosheng Hu, Jiayang Li
venue: arXiv:2610.17099v1 [cs.AR]
id: 2610.17099
whatRead: Full text via arXiv HTML (main body and references; no code, no supplementary material)
verdict: The paper reports a working KV260 prototype that trains a four-layer CNN and its local classifiers from scratch with integer arithmetic on one shared matrix engine. The energy advantage over an integer-method Jetson run is measured under different power boundaries, and the mechanism claims (compact errors, tile folding, direct conversion) rest on logical-capacity accounting and isolated RTL tests rather than board-level ablations. My read is that it is credible as a feasibility demonstration and weak as attribution of gains to individual mechanisms.
overallConfidence: 76
---

## Motivation (Confidence: 80%)
The paper argues that on-device training is bottlenecked by computation, retained forward activations, and data movement. Local learning (a per-layer objective, no cross-layer error propagation) and integer arithmetic each address part of this, and NITRO-D and FF-INT8 show integer local training works on GPUs. The authors say the open question is hardware: how convolution and local-classifier operations should share resources, and how saved operands, gradients, and quantization scales should be stored and reused. They add that local learning still needs operand retention, gradient accumulation, and scale selection.

My read: the gap is real and specific. Earlier FPGA trainers share engines across forward, backward, and weight-gradient passes, but not across local-classifier work. The motivation is less convincing on the energy side, since the baseline is the authors' own integer implementation on a GPU.

## Core idea (Confidence: 85%)
Each of four convolutional layers (alternating standard and grouped 3×3, bias-free) has a trainable local classifier on its quantized ReLU outputs. Each channel is averaged over four spatial regions, giving \(4C\) features per layer rather than \(HWC\). With regional features \(F\), pre-update classifier weights \(V_{\mathrm{pre}}\), and classifier errors \(E\), all three classifier computations are matrix products that run on the same engine as the convolutions:

$$Z = F V_{\mathrm{pre}}^{T},\qquad G_V = E^{T}F,\qquad E_F = E V_{\mathrm{pre}}$$

Regional errors \(E_F\) stay compact in DDR. They are broadcast over their region on chip only when the convolutional weight gradient needs spatial errors. The \(1/A\) averaging factor is folded into the exponent, and a ReLU mask is applied. Training finishes one layer before the next, so saved inputs can be reused afterward.

Numerics are shared-exponent INT8 with INT32 accumulation. Conversion divides every code in a tensor by one power of two:

$$q_i' = \operatorname{sat}_8\!\big(\operatorname{RN}(q_i 2^{-s})\big),\qquad e' = e + s,\quad s\ge 0$$

Convolutional output scales come from history rather than the current batch. After eight warmup batches, a histogram of raw INT32 magnitudes gives the 99.9th-percentile bin bound \(U_t\), a candidate exponent \(h_t\), and a target that is the maximum over the previous four batches:

$$h_t = e_{\mathrm{raw},t} + \min\{s\ge 0 : U_t \le 127\cdot 2^{s}\},\qquad e_{\mathrm{target},t}=\max_{i=1,\dots,4} h_{t-i}$$

This lets INT32-to-INT8 conversion happen directly as outputs stream out, while the histogram observes the raw codes in parallel.

Weight gradients are normalized per tensor by GB5, which targets a 5-bit magnitude, \(s_G=\max(0,\,b(\max|G|)-5)\). GB5 needs the complete batch gradient range before any update. The hardware therefore folds 32 partial gradient tensors (for \(B=256\), eight-sample groups) one 8×8 tile at a time through a 48-bit accumulator of 384 bytes of logical storage, with two 256-byte prefetch buffers.

The engine has eight pods of 8×8 INT8 outer-product tiles, so 512 MAC/cycle and a 102.4 GMAC/s peak at 200 MHz. The final classifier uses an INT16 master weight state \(M=16W\) so updates smaller than one INT8 step are kept. Classification errors use an FPGA-friendly adaptation of NITI's TiLoss, with lookup tables, one shared 28-fractional-bit reciprocal per sample, and a fixed \(2^{15}\) polynomial scale for small exponents.

<figure data-n="1" data-a="S2.F1"><figcaption>Fig. 1: One CNN layer and its local supervision. Dashed paths retain operands; the local head also updates V. No convolutional input gradient is sent to earlier layers.</figcaption></figure>

<figure data-n="2" data-a="S3.F2"><figcaption>Fig. 2: Hardware organization. Convolution and classifier operations reuse the matrix engine. Regional errors expand in operand supply; numerical and output units share DDR access. Control is dashed; data connections are solid.</figcaption></figure>

## Distinguishing factor (Confidence: 70%)
Against integer training algorithms (NITI, NITRO-D, FF-INT8): these are algorithm-level, mostly GPU results. The paper reuses NITI's TiLoss structure and its code-domain updates, and its new content is the hardware mapping and the FPGA-oriented rewrite of the loss (lookup tables, shared reciprocal, bounded polynomial). The training rules themselves are largely inherited.

Against FPGA backpropagation accelerators (EF-Train, Venkataramanaiah et al., Shao et al.): these share compute across forward, backward, and weight-gradient passes. Here the shared engine also absorbs local-classifier score, feature-error, and classifier-gradient products (Table I), and local supervision removes the cross-layer input-gradient path. The comparison in Table IV mixes six-layer narrow backprop networks with a four-layer wider, partly grouped local-learning network, so accuracy and energy are not like-for-like, and the paper says so.

Against historical-scaling work (Flexpoint, Yamaguchi et al.): the idea of using earlier batches to pick scales is adopted. The paper's specific choices are histogram percentiles, a four-batch window, and an eight-batch warmup, plus the hardware that makes direct conversion possible. Saturation is allowed and no clipping rate is guaranteed.

vs. their earlier version: the paper does not describe an earlier version in the text I read, and none was fetched.

## Evaluation and conclusions (Confidence: 78%)
Complete training on the KV260 at 200 MHz reaches 76.57% on CIFAR-10 (50 epochs) and 91.85% on SVHN (60 epochs), at about 4.20 W mean VCC_SOM power. Against the same integer method on a Jetson Orin Nano (25 W mode), measured energy is 40.4% and 42.1% lower, with 1.86× and 1.82× longer runtime. My read: this is the headline claim, and the boundaries differ. The FPGA measures VCC_SOM and the Jetson measures VDD_IN, with no idle subtraction, and the Jetson integer path is custom numerics in PyTorch, not an optimized kernel. The paper's own Jetson table shows Local FP32 on the Jetson at lower energy than the FPGA's CIFAR-10 figure, again under different boundaries. The FPGA's energy advantage therefore holds against the integer-on-GPU implementation specifically, not against a good GPU local-learning baseline. Functional agreement is supported by matched cumulative training predictions and final test counts between FPGA and Jetson integer runs, plus selected first-batch and checkpoint-restore state checks. The authors correctly say this is not every intermediate value.

Effective throughput is 31.22 and 31.57 GMAC/s, about 30% of the 102.4 peak. It is computed as nominal algorithmic MACs divided by measured PL time, and PL time is about 97% of wall time. The first two convolutions have short reductions (36 and 72 steps) against 128 result transfers for eight tiles, so result bandwidth is flagged as a constraint. My read: the number is honest about low utilization, but the stall attribution is a hypothesis, since the counters do not separate memory stalls from result backpressure.

Storage and conversion results are the mechanism evidence. Compact regional errors take 64 to 256 KiB per layer against 8 to 16 MiB for expanded INT8 spatial errors, and tile folding replaces a 432 KiB resident accumulator (conv 3) with 384 bytes plus 512 bytes of prefetch. Direct conversion avoids 448 MiB of raw-output write/read payload per full batch after warmup. In an isolated RTL test (262,144 INT32 values), direct conversion takes 50.0% fewer cycles with always-ready interfaces and 63.8% fewer under a fixed throttling model. My read: these are logical capacities and payload counts, not measured DDR traffic or peak live storage, and the reserved DDR workspace stays at 161.22 MiB. The module test excludes convolution and external scratch control. These mechanisms are plausible but not demonstrated on the board.

Software accuracy on the Jetson is somewhat surprising. Integer local (76.57% on CIFAR-10) beats Local FP32 (74.43%) but trails FP32 backprop (79.87%). Scheduled freezing cuts training time 28.4% and 32.3% while raising accuracy 6.38 and 2.03 points in a within-study comparison. My read: the integer-over-FP32 result mostly reflects differing training recipes (Adam and cross-entropy versus integer rules and scaling), which the authors partly acknowledge. The freezing gain means the schedule is part of the reported result, not a free speedup.

The paper's conclusion is that complete integer local CNN training works with shared arithmetic, that compact errors, folding, and historical scales reduce buffering, and that energy is lower though runtime is longer. It also states the evidence covers one FPGA seed, logical storage savings, and isolated conversion tests. My read: the evidence supports the feasibility and the energy-versus-Jetson-integer claim under stated caveats, and does not yet support attributing the gains to specific mechanisms.

## In case you missed it (Confidence: 75%)
- [pos] The TiLoss rewrite is concrete: lookup tables for score exponents above -7, one reciprocal with 28 fractional bits shared across all classes per sample, a fixed \(2^{15}\) scale for exponents at or below -10, and stochastic division by 16 to reach INT8 errors.
- [neu] Convolutional and local-classifier weights are updated in the code domain without aligning the gradient exponent to the fixed weight exponent; only the final classifier gets an INT16 master state (\(M=16W\), clipped to ±2032) that preserves sub-step updates.
- [neg] The 64 MiB DDR buffer for raw outputs during warmup stays reserved afterward, so direct conversion removes traffic but not reserved capacity; total reserved DDR workspace stays at 161.22 MiB.
- [neu] The histogram unit is 8 banks of 33 counters of 25 bits in LUTRAM with HLS initiation interval of one; the PS merges bank counts, checks the output count, and keeps the scale history. At evaluation, each layer's target is fixed to the maximum of its last four training candidates.
- [neu] The deployed configuration bakes in layer freezing after 20/20/30/40 epochs, gradients with max magnitude at or below 1 are suppressed, and the final classifier's learning-rate shift changes from 24 to 25 after epoch 40.
- [neg] The PS supplies all random values (dropout, stochastic rounding) through DDR and maintains scale history, so training is not fully self-contained on the PL; the SVHN run also drops one training image to fit eight-sample grouping.

The same integer method on Jetson is a controlled comparison, but power boundaries differ (VCC_SOM vs VDD_IN, no idle subtraction) and the Jetson integer path is unoptimized custom code. The FPGA literature table mixes different networks, precisions, and tool-estimated power, and the authors disclaim an energy ranking. | 72% |

---
title: LocalTrain: Integer-Only Local Learning for Energy-Efficient CNN Training on Edge FPGAs
authors: Shiqi Ding, Xuanyu Liang, Xinyang Liu, Boyang Li, Zhiqiang Que, Guosheng Hu, Jiayang Li
venue: arXiv:2610.17099v1 [cs.AR]
id: 2610.17099
whatRead: Full text via arXiv HTML (main body and references; no code, no supplementary material)
verdict: The paper reports a working KV260 prototype that trains a four-layer CNN and its local classifiers from scratch with integer arithmetic on one shared matrix engine. The energy advantage over an integer-method Jetson run is measured under different power boundaries, and the mechanism claims (compact errors, tile folding, direct conversion) rest on logical-capacity accounting and isolated RTL tests rather than board-level ablations. My read is that it is credible as a feasibility demonstration and weak as attribution of gains to individual mechanisms.
overallConfidence: 76
---

## Motivation (Confidence: 80%)
The paper argues that on-device training is bottlenecked by computation, retained forward activations, and data movement. Local learning (a per-layer objective, no cross-layer error propagation) and integer arithmetic each address part of this, and NITRO-D and FF-INT8 show integer local training works on GPUs. The authors say the open question is hardware: how convolution and local-classifier operations should share resources, and how saved operands, gradients, and quantization scales should be stored and reused. They add that local learning still needs operand retention, gradient accumulation, and scale selection.

My read: the gap is real and specific. Earlier FPGA trainers share engines across forward, backward, and weight-gradient passes, but not across local-classifier work. The motivation is less convincing on the energy side, since the baseline is the authors' own integer implementation on a GPU.

## Core idea (Confidence: 85%)
Each of four convolutional layers (alternating standard and grouped 3×3, bias-free) has a trainable local classifier on its quantized ReLU outputs. Each channel is averaged over four spatial regions, giving \(4C\) features per layer rather than \(HWC\). With regional features \(F\), pre-update classifier weights \(V_{\mathrm{pre}}\), and classifier errors \(E\), all three classifier computations are matrix products that run on the same engine as the convolutions:

$$Z = F V_{\mathrm{pre}}^{T},\qquad G_V = E^{T}F,\qquad E_F = E V_{\mathrm{pre}}$$

Regional errors \(E_F\) stay compact in DDR. They are broadcast over their region on chip only when the convolutional weight gradient needs spatial errors. The \(1/A\) averaging factor is folded into the exponent, and a ReLU mask is applied. Training finishes one layer before the next, so saved inputs can be reused afterward.

Numerics are shared-exponent INT8 with INT32 accumulation. Conversion divides every code in a tensor by one power of two:

$$q_i' = \operatorname{sat}_8\!\big(\operatorname{RN}(q_i 2^{-s})\big),\qquad e' = e + s,\quad s\ge 0$$

Convolutional output scales come from history rather than the current batch. After eight warmup batches, a histogram of raw INT32 magnitudes gives the 99.9th-percentile bin bound \(U_t\), a candidate exponent \(h_t\), and a target that is the maximum over the previous four batches:

$$h_t = e_{\mathrm{raw},t} + \min\{s\ge 0 : U_t \le 127\cdot 2^{s}\},\qquad e_{\mathrm{target},t}=\max_{i=1,\dots,4} h_{t-i}$$

This lets INT32-to-INT8 conversion happen directly as outputs stream out, while the histogram observes the raw codes in parallel.

Weight gradients are normalized per tensor by GB5, which targets a 5-bit magnitude, \(s_G=\max(0,\,b(\max|G|)-5)\). GB5 needs the complete batch gradient range before any update. The hardware therefore folds 32 partial gradient tensors (for \(B=256\), eight-sample groups) one 8×8 tile at a time through a 48-bit accumulator of 384 bytes of logical storage, with two 256-byte prefetch buffers.

The engine has eight pods of 8×8 INT8 outer-product tiles, so 512 MAC/cycle and a 102.4 GMAC/s peak at 200 MHz. The final classifier uses an INT16 master weight state \(M=16W\) so updates smaller than one INT8 step are kept. Classification errors use an FPGA-friendly adaptation of NITI's TiLoss, with lookup tables, one shared 28-fractional-bit reciprocal per sample, and a fixed \(2^{15}\) polynomial scale for small exponents.

<figure data-n="1" data-a="S2.F1"><figcaption>Fig. 1: One CNN layer and its local supervision. Dashed paths retain operands; the local head also updates V. No convolutional input gradient is sent to earlier layers.</figcaption></figure>

<figure data-n="2" data-a="S3.F2"><figcaption>Fig. 2: Hardware organization. Convolution and classifier operations reuse the matrix engine. Regional errors expand in operand supply; numerical and output units share DDR access. Control is dashed; data connections are solid.</figcaption></figure>

## Distinguishing factor (Confidence: 70%)
Against integer training algorithms (NITI, NITRO-D, FF-INT8): these are algorithm-level, mostly GPU results. The paper reuses NITI's TiLoss structure and its code-domain updates, and its new content is the hardware mapping and the FPGA-oriented rewrite of the loss (lookup tables, shared reciprocal, bounded polynomial). The training rules themselves are largely inherited.

Against FPGA backpropagation accelerators (EF-Train, Venkataramanaiah et al., Shao et al.): these share compute across forward, backward, and weight-gradient passes. Here the shared engine also absorbs local-classifier score, feature-error, and classifier-gradient products (Table I), and local supervision removes the cross-layer input-gradient path. The comparison in Table IV mixes six-layer narrow backprop networks with a four-layer wider, partly grouped local-learning network, so accuracy and energy are not like-for-like, and the paper says so.

Against historical-scaling work (Flexpoint, Yamaguchi et al.): the idea of using earlier batches to pick scales is adopted. The paper's specific choices are histogram percentiles, a four-batch window, and an eight-batch warmup, plus the hardware that makes direct conversion possible. Saturation is allowed and no clipping rate is guaranteed.

vs. their earlier version: the paper does not describe an earlier version in the text I read, and none was fetched.

## Evaluation and conclusions (Confidence: 78%)
Complete training on the KV260 at 200 MHz reaches 76.57% on CIFAR-10 (50 epochs) and 91.85% on SVHN (60 epochs), at about 4.20 W mean VCC_SOM power. Against the same integer method on a Jetson Orin Nano (25 W mode), measured energy is 40.4% and 42.1% lower, with 1.86× and 1.82× longer runtime. My read: this is the headline claim, and the boundaries differ. The FPGA measures VCC_SOM and the Jetson measures VDD_IN, with no idle subtraction, and the Jetson integer path is custom numerics in PyTorch, not an optimized kernel. The paper's own Jetson table shows Local FP32 on the Jetson at lower energy than the FPGA's CIFAR-10 figure, again under different boundaries. The FPGA's energy advantage therefore holds against the integer-on-GPU implementation specifically, not against a good GPU local-learning baseline. Functional agreement is supported by matched cumulative training predictions and final test counts between FPGA and Jetson integer runs, plus selected first-batch and checkpoint-restore state checks. The authors correctly say this is not every intermediate value.

Effective throughput is 31.22 and 31.57 GMAC/s, about 30% of the 102.4 peak. It is computed as nominal algorithmic MACs divided by measured PL time, and PL time is about 97% of wall time. The first two convolutions have short reductions (36 and 72 steps) against 128 result transfers for eight tiles, so result bandwidth is flagged as a constraint. My read: the number is honest about low utilization, but the stall attribution is a hypothesis, since the counters do not separate memory stalls from result backpressure.

Storage and conversion results are the mechanism evidence. Compact regional errors take 64 to 256 KiB per layer against 8 to 16 MiB for expanded INT8 spatial errors, and tile folding replaces a 432 KiB resident accumulator (conv 3) with 384 bytes plus 512 bytes of prefetch. Direct conversion avoids 448 MiB of raw-output write/read payload per full batch after warmup. In an isolated RTL test (262,144 INT32 values), direct conversion takes 50.0% fewer cycles with always-ready interfaces and 63.8% fewer under a fixed throttling model. My read: these are logical capacities and payload counts, not measured DDR traffic or peak live storage, and the reserved DDR workspace stays at 161.22 MiB. The module test excludes convolution and external scratch control. These mechanisms are plausible but not demonstrated on the board.

Software accuracy on the Jetson is somewhat surprising. Integer local (76.57% on CIFAR-10) beats Local FP32 (74.43%) but trails FP32 backprop (79.87%). Scheduled freezing cuts training time 28.4% and 32.3% while raising accuracy 6.38 and 2.03 points in a within-study comparison. My read: the integer-over-FP32 result mostly reflects differing training recipes (Adam and cross-entropy versus integer rules and scaling), which the authors partly acknowledge. The freezing gain means the schedule is part of the reported result, not a free speedup.

The paper's conclusion is that complete integer local CNN training works with shared arithmetic, that compact errors, folding, and historical scales reduce buffering, and that energy is lower though runtime is longer. It also states the evidence covers one FPGA seed, logical storage savings, and isolated conversion tests. My read: the evidence supports the feasibility and the energy-versus-Jetson-integer claim under stated caveats, and does not yet support attributing the gains to specific mechanisms.

## In case you missed it (Confidence: 75%)
- [pos] The TiLoss rewrite is concrete: lookup tables for score exponents above -7, one reciprocal with 28 fractional bits shared across all classes per sample, a fixed \(2^{15}\) scale for exponents at or below -10, and stochastic division by 16 to reach INT8 errors.
- [neu] Convolutional and local-classifier weights are updated in the code domain without aligning the gradient exponent to the fixed weight exponent; only the final classifier gets an INT16 master state (\(M=16W\), clipped to ±2032) that preserves sub-step updates.
- [neg] The 64 MiB DDR buffer for raw outputs during warmup stays reserved afterward, so direct conversion removes traffic but not reserved capacity; total reserved DDR workspace stays at 161.22 MiB.
- [neu] The histogram unit is 8 banks of 33 counters of 25 bits in LUTRAM with HLS initiation interval of one; the PS merges bank counts, checks the output count, and keeps the scale history. At evaluation, each layer's target is fixed to the maximum of its last four training candidates.
- [neu] The deployed configuration bakes in layer freezing after 20/20/30/40 epochs, gradients with max magnitude at or below 1 are suppressed, and the final classifier's learning-rate shift changes from 24 to 25 after epoch 40.
- [neg] The PS supplies all random values (dropout, stochastic rounding) through DDR and maintains scale history, so training is not fully self-contained on the PL; the SVHN run also drops one training image to fit eight-sample grouping.

The same integer method on Jetson is a controlled comparison, but power boundaries differ (VCC_SOM vs VDD_IN, no idle subtraction) and the Jetson integer path is unoptimized custom code.
